\sin^{l}(x)\approx\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{l}\sum_{m^{\prime}=\lceil l/2\rceil-M_{2}}^{\lfloor l/2\rfloor+M_{2}}(-1)^{m^{\prime}}{l\choose m^{\prime}}e^{ix(2m^{\prime}-l)},

(B.53)

which induces an error of
\epsilon_{2}/2
for the choice

M_{2}\geq\left\lceil\log\left(\frac{4}{\epsilon_{2}}\right)\sqrt{(2\log{\delta_{u}^{-1}})^{-1}}\right\rceil.

This can be seen by using Chernoff’s inequality for sums of binomial coefficients, i.e.,

\sum_{m^{\prime}=\lceil l/2+M_{2}\rceil}^{l}2^{-l}{l\choose m^{\prime}}\leq e^{-\frac{2M_{2}^{2}}{l}},

and chosing
M
appropriately.
Finally, defining
f(z):=\arcsin(\sin(z\pi/2)/(\pi/2))
, as well as
\tilde{f}_{1}:=\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{K_{2}}b_{k^{\prime}}\sin^{2k^{\prime}+1}(z\pi/2)
and

\tilde{f}_{2}(z):=\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{K_{2}}b_{k^{\prime}}\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{l}\sum_{m^{\prime}=\lceil l/2\rceil-M_{2}}^{\lfloor l/2\rfloor+M_{2}}(-1)^{m^{\prime}}{l\choose m^{\prime}}e^{ix(2m^{\prime}-l)},

(B.54)

and observing that

\left\lVert f-\tilde{f}_{2}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert f-\tilde{f}_{1}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\tilde{f}_{1}-\tilde{f}_{2}\right\rVert_{\infty},

yields the final error of
\epsilon_{2}
for the approximation

z\approx\tilde{z}=\sum_{m^{\prime}}\tilde{c}_{m^{\prime}}e^{i\pi m^{\prime}z/2}
.
∎

Note that this immediately leads to an
\epsilon_{2}
error in the spectral norm for the approximation

\left\lVert\sigma_{v}-\sum_{m^{\prime}=-M_{2}}^{M_{2}}\tilde{c}_{m^{\prime}}e^{i\pi m^{\prime}\sigma_{v}/2}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\epsilon_{2},

(B.55)

where
\sigma_{v}
is the reduced density matrix.

Since our final goal is to estimate
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\partial_{\theta}\rho\log\sigma_{v}\right)
, with a variety of
\sigma_{v}(\theta_{j})
using the divided difference approach, we also need to bound the error in this estimate which we introduce with the above approximations.
Bounding the derivative with respect to the remainder can be done by using the truncated series expansion and bounding the gradient of the remainder. This yields the following result.

Lemma 21
.

For the of the parameters
M_{1},M_{2},K_{1},L,\mu,\Delta,s
given in eq. (
B.89
-
B.96
), and
\rho,\sigma_{v}
being two density matrices, we can estimate the gradient of the relative entropy such that

\left\lvert\partial_{\theta}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho\log\sigma_{v}\right)-\partial_{\theta}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}\right)\right\rvert\leq\epsilon,

(B.56)

where the function
\partial_{\theta}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}\right)
evaluated at
\theta
is defined as

\mathrm{Re}\left[\sum_{m=-M_{1}}^{M_{1}}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-M_{2}}^{M_{2}}\frac{ic_{m}\tilde{c}_{m^{\prime}}m\pi}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\mu}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mu,j}(\theta)\mathbb{E}_{s\in[0,1]}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho e^{\frac{is\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}e^{\frac{i\pi m^{\prime}}{2}\sigma_{v}(\theta_{j})}e^{\frac{i(1-s)\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}\right)\right]\right]

(B.57)

The gradient can hence be approximated to error
\epsilon
with
O(\text{poly}(M_{1},M_{2},K_{1},L,s,\Delta,\mu))
computation on a classical computer and using only the Hadamard test, Gibbs state preparation and LCU on a quantum device.

Notably the expression in (
B.57
) can now be evaluated with a quantum-classical hybrid device by evaluating each term in the trace separately via a Hadamard test and, since the number of terms is only polynomial, and then evaluating the whole sum efficiently on a classical device.

Proof.

For the proof we perform the following steps.
Let
\sigma_{i}(\rho)
be the singular values of
\rho
, which are equivalently the eigenvalues since
\rho
is Hermitian.
Then observe that the gradient can be separated in different terms, i.e., let
\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}^{s}\sigma_{v}
be the approximation as given in (
B.57
) for a finite sample of the expectation values
\mathbb{E}_{s}
, then we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\partial_{\theta}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho\log\sigma_{v}\right)-\partial_{\theta}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}^{s}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}\right)\right\rvert\leq

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}(\rho)\cdot\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}[\log\sigma_{v}-\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}^{s}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}]\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}(\rho)\cdot\left(\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}[\log\sigma_{v}-\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\sigma_{v}]\right\rVert\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}[\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\sigma_{v}-\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}]\right\rVert+\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}[\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma_{v}}-\log^{s}_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}]\right\rVert\right)

(B.58)

where the second step follows from the Von-Neumann trace inequality and the terms are (1) the error in approximating the logarithm, (2) the error introduced by the divided difference and the approximation of
\sigma_{v}
as a Fourier-like series, and (3) is the finite sampling approximation error.
We can now bound the different term separately, and start with the first part which is in general harder to estimate.
We partition the bound in three terms, corresponding to the three different approximations taken above.

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}[\log\sigma_{v}-\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\sigma_{v}]\right\rVert\leq

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}\sum_{k=K_{1}+1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}\sigma_{v}^{k}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}\sum_{l=L}^{\infty}b_{l}^{(k)}\sin^{l}(\sigma_{v}\pi/2)\right\rVert

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}\sum_{l=L}^{\infty}b_{l}^{(k)}\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{l}\sum_{m\in[0,\lceil l/2\rceil-M_{1}]\cup[\lfloor l/2\rfloor+M_{1},l]}(-1)^{m}e^{i(2m-l)\sigma_{v}\pi/2}\right\rVert

The first term can be bound in the following way:

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=K_{1}+1}^{\infty}\lVert\sigma_{v}\rVert^{k-1}=\frac{\lVert\sigma_{v}\rVert^{K_{1}}}{1-\lVert\sigma_{v}\rVert},

(B.59)

and, assuming
\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\right\rVert<1
, we hence can set

K_{1}\geq\log((1-\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\right\rVert)\epsilon/9)/\log(\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\right\rVert)

(B.60)

appropriately in order to achieve an
\epsilon/9
error. The second term can be bound by assuming that
\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\pi\right\rVert<1
, and chosing

L\geq\log\frac{\left(\frac{\epsilon}{9\pi K\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert}\right)}{\log(\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\right\rVert\pi)},

which we derive by observing that

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=L}^{\infty}b_{l}^{(k)}l\left\lVert\sin^{l-1}(\sigma_{v}\pi/2)\right\rVert\cdot\left\lVert\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert

(B.61)

\displaystyle<\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=L+1}^{\infty}b_{l}^{(k)}\pi\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\pi\right\rVert^{l-1}\cdot\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert

(B.62)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{k}\pi\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\pi\right\rVert^{L}\cdot\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert,

(B.63)

where we used in the second step that
l<2^{l}
.
Finally, the last term can be bound similarly, which yields

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=1}^{L}b_{l}^{(k)}e^{-2(M_{1})^{2}/l}\cdot l\cdot\frac{\pi}{2}\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert

(B.64)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{L}{k}\sum_{l=1}^{L}b_{l}^{(k)}e^{-2(M_{1})^{2}/L}\frac{\pi}{2}\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert

(B.65)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{L}{k}e^{-2(M_{1})^{2}/L}\frac{\pi}{2}\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert\leq\frac{KL\pi}{2}e^{-2(M_{1})^{2}/L}\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert,

(B.66)

and we can hence chose

M_{1}\geq\sqrt{L\log\left(\frac{9\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert K_{1}L\pi}{2\epsilon}\right)}

in order to decrease the error to
\epsilon/3
for the first term in (
B.3
).

For the second term, first note that with the notation we chose,
\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}[\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\sigma_{v}-\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}]\right\rVert
is the difference between the log-approximation where the gradient of
\sigma_{v}
is still exact, i.e., (
B.48
), and the version where we approximate the gradient via divided differences and the linear combination of unitaries, given in (
B.57
).
Recall that the first level approximation was given by

\sum_{m=-M_{1}}^{M_{1}}\frac{ic_{m}m\pi}{2}\int_{0}^{1}ds\ \mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho e^{\frac{is\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}e^{\frac{i(1-s)\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}\right),

where we went from the expectation value formulation back to the integral formulation to avoid consideration of potential errors due to sampling.

Bounding the difference hence yields one term from the divided difference approximation of the gradient and an error from the Fourier series, which we can both bound separately. Denoting
\partial\tilde{p}(\theta_{k})/\partial\theta
as the divided difference and the LCU approximation of the Fourier series
1
1

1

which effectively means that we approximate the coefficients of the interpolation polynomial
, and with
\partial p(\theta_{k})/\partial\theta
the divided difference without approximation via the Fourier series, we hence have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}[\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\sigma_{v}-\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}]\right\rVert\leq

(B.67)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\sum_{m=-M_{1}}^{M_{1}}\frac{ic_{m}m\pi}{2}\int_{0}^{1}ds\ \mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho e^{\frac{is\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}\left(\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}-\frac{\partial\tilde{p}(\theta_{k})}{\partial\theta}\right)e^{\frac{i(1-s)\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}\right)\right\rvert

(B.68)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{1}\pi\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}ds\ \sum_{i}\sigma_{i}(\rho)\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}-\frac{\partial\tilde{p}(\theta_{k})}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert

(B.69)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{1}\pi\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}ds\ \sum_{i}\sigma_{i}(\rho)\left(\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}-\frac{\partial p(\theta_{k})}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\frac{\partial p(\theta_{k})}{\partial\theta}-\frac{\partial\tilde{p}(\theta_{k})}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert\right)

(B.70)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\pi}{2}\int_{0}^{1}ds\ \sum_{i}\sigma_{i}(\rho)\left(\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{\mu+1}\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta^{\mu+1}}\right\rVert\left(\frac{\Delta}{\mu-1}\right)^{\mu}\frac{\max_{k}(\mu-k)!}{(\mu+1)!}+\sum_{j=0}^{\mu}\lvert\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mu,j}(\theta_{j})\rvert\left\lVert\sigma_{v}-\tilde{\sigma}_{v}\right\rVert\right)

(B.71)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\pi}{2}\left(\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{\mu+1}\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta^{\mu+1}}\right\rVert\left(\frac{\Delta}{\mu-1}\right)^{\mu}\frac{\mu!}{(\mu+1)!}+\mu\left\lVert\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mu,j}(\theta_{j})\right\rVert_{\infty}\epsilon_{2}\right),

(B.72)

where
\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}=\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}1/k
, and we used in the last step the results of Lemma
20
. Under appropriate assumptions on the grid-spacing for the divided difference scheme
\Delta
and the number of evaluated points
\mu
as well as a bound on the
\mu+1
-st derivative of
\sigma_{v}
w.r.t.
\theta
, we can hence also bound this error.
In order to do so, we need to analyze the
\mu+1
-st derivative of
\sigma_{v}=\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]/Z
with
Z=\mathrm{Tr}\left(e^{-H}\right)
.
For this we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{\mu+1}\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta^{\mu+1}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{p=1}^{\mu+1}{\mu+1\choose p}\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{p}\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]}{\partial\theta^{p}}\right\rVert\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{\mu+1-p}Z^{-1}}{\partial\theta^{\mu+1-p}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\mu+1}\max_{p}\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{p}\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]}{\partial\theta^{p}}\right\rVert\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{\mu+1-p}Z^{-1}}{\partial\theta^{\mu+1-p}}\right\rVert

(B.73)

We have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{p}\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]}{\partial\theta^{p}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{dim}(H_{h})\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{q}e^{-H}}{\partial\theta^{q}}\right\rVert

(B.74)

where
\mathrm{dim}(H_{h})=2^{n_{h}}
. In order to bound this, we take advantage of the infinitesimal expansion of the exponent, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{q}e^{-H}}{\partial\theta^{q}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{q}}{\partial\theta^{q}}\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\prod_{j=1}^{r}e^{-H/r}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{\partial^{q}e^{-H/r}}{\partial\theta^{q}}\prod_{j=2}^{r}e^{-H/r}+\frac{\partial^{q-1}e^{-H/r}}{\partial\theta^{q-1}}\frac{\partial e^{-H/r}}{\partial\theta}\prod_{j=3}^{r}e^{-H/r}+\ldots\right)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left\lVert\frac{\partial H/r}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert^{q}\cdot r^{q}+O\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\right)\left\lVert e^{-H}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\frac{\partial H}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert^{q}\left\lVert e^{-H}\right\rVert,

(B.75)

where the last step follows from the fact that we have
r^{q}
terms and that we used that the error introduced by the commutations above will be of
O(1/r)
.
Observing that
\partial_{\theta_{i}}H=\partial_{\theta_{i}}\sum_{j}\theta_{j}H_{j}=H_{i}
and assuming that
\lambda_{max}
is the largest singular eigenvalue of
H
, we can hence bound this by
\lambda_{max}^{q}\left\lVert e^{-H}\right\rVert
.

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{p}\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]}{\partial\theta^{p}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{dim}(H_{h})\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{q}e^{-H}}{\partial\theta^{q}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{max}^{p}\mathrm{dim}(H_{h})\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]\right\rVert,

(B.76)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{\mu+1-p}Z^{-1}}{\partial\theta^{\mu+1-p}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(\mu+1-p)!\lvert\lambda_{max}\rvert^{\mu+1-p}}{Z^{\mu+2-p}}\mathrm{Tr}\left(e^{-H}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\mu+1-p}{eZ}\right)^{\mu+1-p}\frac{e}{Z}\lvert\lambda_{max}\rvert^{\mu+1-p}\mathrm{Tr}\left(e^{-H}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mu+1-p}{eZ}\right)^{\mu+1-p}e\lvert\lambda_{max}\rvert^{\mu+1-p}

(B.77)

We can therefore find a bound for (
B.3
) as

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{\mu+1}\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta^{\mu+1}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq e2^{\mu+1+n_{h}}\lambda_{max}^{\mu+1}\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]\right\rVert\max_{p}\left(\frac{\mu+1-p}{eZ}\right)^{\mu+1-p}.

(B.78)

Plugging this result into the bound from above yields

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}[\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\sigma_{v}-\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}]\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\pi}{2}\left(e2^{\mu+1+n_{h}}\lambda_{max}^{\mu+1}\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]\right\rVert\max_{p}\left(\frac{\mu+1-p}{eZ}\right)^{\mu+1-p}\left(\frac{\Delta}{\mu-1}\right)^{\mu}\frac{1}{\mu+1}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\pi}{2}\left(\mu\left\lVert\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mu,j}(\theta_{j})\right\rVert_{\infty}\epsilon_{2}\right),

(B.79)

Note that under the reasonable assumption that
2\leq\mu\ll Z
, the maximum is achieved for
p=\mu+1
, and we hence obtain the upper bound

\displaystyle\frac{M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\pi}{2}\left(2^{n_{h}}e(2\lvert\lambda_{max}\rvert)^{\mu+1}\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]\right\rVert\left(\frac{\Delta}{\mu-1}\right)^{\mu}\frac{1}{\mu+1}+\mu\left\lVert\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mu,j}(\theta_{j})\right\rVert_{\infty}\epsilon_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\pi}{2}\left(2^{n_{h}}e(2\lvert\lambda_{max}\rvert)^{\mu+1}\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]\right\rVert\left(\frac{\Delta}{\mu-1}\right)^{\mu}+\mu\left\lVert\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mu,j}(\theta_{j})\right\rVert_{\infty}\epsilon_{2}\right),

(B.80)

and we can hence obtain a bound on
\mu
, the grid point number, in order to achieve an error of
\epsilon/6>0
for the former term, which is given by

\mu\geq(\lvert\lambda_{max}\rvert\Delta)\exp\left(W\left(\frac{\log\left(2^{n_{h}}\frac{6M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}e^{2}\lvert\lambda_{max}\rvert\pi\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]\right\rVert}{\epsilon}\right)}{2\lambda_{max}\Delta}\right)\right),

(B.81)

where
W
is the Lambert function, also known as product-log function, which generally grows slower than the logarithm in the asymptotic limit.
Note that
\mu
can hence be lower bounded by

\mu\geq n_{h}+\log\left(\frac{6M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}e^{2}\lvert\lambda_{max}\rvert\pi\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]\right\rVert}{\epsilon}\right):=n_{h}+\log\left(\frac{M_{1}\Lambda}{\epsilon}\right).

(B.82)

For convenience, let us choose
\epsilon
such that
n_{h}+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)
is an integer. We do this simply to avoid having to keep track of ceiling or floor functions in the following discussion where we will choose
\mu=n_{h}+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)
.

For the second part, we will bound the derivative of the Lagrangian interpolation polynomial.
First, note that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mu,j}(\theta)=\sum_{l=0;l\neq j}^{\mu}\left(\prod_{k=0;k\neq j,l}\frac{\theta-\theta_{k}}{\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}}\right)\frac{1}{\theta_{j}-\theta_{l}}
for a chosen discretization of the space such that
\theta_{k}-\theta_{j}=(k-j)\Delta/\mu
can be bound by using a central difference formula, such that we use an uneven number of points (i.e. we take
\mu=2\kappa+1
for positive integer
\kappa
) and chose the point
m
at which we evaluate the gradient as the central point of the mesh.
Note that in this case he have that for
\mu\geq 5
and
\theta_{m}
being the parameters at the midpoint of the stencil

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\mu,j}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\neq j\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\neq j,l\end{subarray}}\frac{|\theta_{m}-\theta_{k}|}{|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|}\frac{1}{\lvert\theta_{l}-\theta_{j}\rvert}\leq\frac{(\kappa!)^{2}}{(\kappa!)^{2}}\frac{\mu}{\Delta}\sum_{l\neq j}\frac{1}{|l-j|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\mu}{\Delta}\sum_{l=1}^{\kappa}\frac{1}{l}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\mu}{\Delta}\left(1+\int_{1}^{\kappa-1}\frac{1}{\ell}\mathrm{d}\ell\right)=\frac{2\mu}{\Delta}\left(1+\log((\mu-3)/2)\right)\leq\frac{5\mu}{\Delta}\log(\mu/2),

(B.83)

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
\mu\geq 5
and
1+\ln(5/2)<(5/2)\ln(5/2)
.
Now, plugging in the
\mu
from (
B.94
), we find that this error is bound by

\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\mu,j}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{5n_{h}+5\log\left(\frac{M_{1}\Lambda}{\epsilon}\right)}{\Delta}\log(n_{h}/2+\log\left(\frac{M_{1}\Lambda}{\epsilon}\right)/2)=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{n_{h}+\log\left(\frac{M_{1}\Lambda}{\epsilon}\right)}{\Delta}\right),

(B.84)

If we want an upper bound of
\epsilon/6
for the second term of the error in (
B.3
), we hence require

\displaystyle\epsilon_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{15M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\pi\mu\left\lVert\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mu,j}(\theta_{j})\right\rVert_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{15M_{1}\|a\|_{1}\pi\left(n_{h}+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)\right)^{2}\log((n_{h}/2)+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)/2)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{15M_{1}\|a\|_{1}\pi\mu^{2}\log((\mu-1)/2)}

(B.85)

We hence obtain that the approximation error due to the divided differences and Fourier series approximation of
\sigma_{v}
is bounded by
\epsilon/3
for the above choice of
\epsilon_{2}
and
\mu
. This bounds the second term in (
B.72
) by
\epsilon/3
.

Finally, we need to take into account the error
\left\lVert\partial_{\theta}[\log_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma_{v}}-\log^{s}_{K_{1},M_{1}}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}]\right\rVert
which we introduce through the sampling process, i.e., through the finite sample estimate of
\mathbb{E}_{s}[\cdot]
here indicated with the superscript
s
over the logarithm.
Note that this error can be bound straight forward by (
B.57
).
We only need to bound the error introduced via the finite amount of samples we take, which is a well-known procedure.
The concrete bounds for the sample error when estimating the expectation value are stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\sigma_{m}
be the sample standard deviation of the random variable

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{s\in[0,1]}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho e^{\frac{is\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}e^{\frac{i\pi m^{\prime}}{2}\sigma_{v}(\theta_{j})}e^{\frac{i(1-s)\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}\right)\right],

(B.86)

such that the sample standard deviation is given by
\sigma_{k}=\frac{\sigma_{m}}{\sqrt{k}}
.
Then with probability at least
1-\delta_{s}
, we can obtain an estimate which is within
\epsilon_{s}\sigma_{m}
of the mean by taking
k=\frac{4}{\epsilon_{s}^{2}}
samples for each sample estimate and taking the median of
O(\log(1/\delta_{s}))
such samples.

Proof.

From Chebyshev’s inequality taking
k=\frac{4}{\epsilon_{s}^{2}}
samples implies that with probability of at least
p=3/4
each of the mean estimates is within
2\sigma_{k}=\epsilon_{s}\sigma_{m}
from the true mean.
Therefore, using standard techniques, we take the median of
O(\log(1/\delta_{s}))
such estimates which gives us with probability
1-\delta_{s}
an estimate of the mean with error at most
\epsilon_{s}\sigma_{m}
, which implies that we need to repeat the procedure
O\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{s}^{2}}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{s}}\right)\right)
times.
∎

We can then bound the error of the sampling step in the final estimate, denoting with
\epsilon_{s}
the sample error, as

\displaystyle\sum_{m=-M_{1}}^{M_{1}}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-M_{2}}^{M_{2}}\left\lvert\frac{ic_{m}c_{m^{\prime}}m\pi}{2}\right\rvert\sum_{j=0}^{\mu}\lvert\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mu,j}(\theta)\rvert\epsilon_{s}\sigma_{m}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}M_{1}\epsilon_{s}\sigma_{m}\pi\mu^{2}\log\left(\frac{\mu}{2}\right)}{\Delta}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{3},

(B.87)

We hence find that for

\displaystyle\epsilon_{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{15\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}M_{1}\sigma_{m}\pi\mu^{2}\log\left(\frac{\mu}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{15M_{1}\|a\|_{1}\sigma_{m}\pi\left(n_{h}+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)\right)^{2}\log((n_{n}/2)+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)/2)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{15M_{1}\|a\|_{1}\sigma_{m}\pi\mu^{2}\log(\mu/2)}

(B.88)

also the last term in (
B.3
) can be bounded by
\epsilon/3
, which together results in an overall error of
\epsilon
for the various approximation steps, which concludes the proof.
∎

Notably all quantities which occure in our bounds are only polynomial in the number of the qubits.
The lower bounds for the choice of parameters are summarized in the following.

\displaystyle M_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{L\log\left(\frac{9\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert K_{1}L\pi}{2\epsilon}\right)}

(B.89)

\displaystyle M_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\lceil\log\left(\frac{4}{\epsilon_{2}}\right)\sqrt{(2\log{\delta_{u}^{-1}})^{-1}}\right\rceil

(B.90)

\displaystyle K_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\log((1-\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\right\rVert)\epsilon/9)/\log(\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\right\rVert)

(B.91)

\displaystyle K_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\log(4/\epsilon_{2})}{\log(\delta_{u}^{-1})}

(B.92)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\log\left(\frac{\epsilon}{9\pi K_{1}\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert}\right)}{\log(\left\lVert\sigma_{v}\right\rVert\pi)}

(B.93)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\geq n_{h}+\log\left(\frac{6M_{1}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}e^{2}\lvert\lambda_{max}\rvert\pi\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{h}\left[e^{-H}\right]\right\rVert}{\epsilon}\right):=n_{h}+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)

(B.94)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{15M_{1}\|a\|_{1}\pi\left(n_{h}+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)\right)^{2}\log((n_{h}/2)+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)/2)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{15M_{1}\|a\|_{1}\pi\mu^{2}\log((\mu-1)/2)}

(B.95)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{15M_{1}\|a\|_{1}\sigma_{m}\pi\left(n_{h}+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)\right)^{2}\log((n_{n}/2)+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)/2)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{15M_{1}\|a\|_{1}\sigma_{m}\pi\mu^{2}\log(\mu/2)}

(B.96)

B.3.1
Operationalising

In the following we will make use of two established subroutines, namely sample based Hamiltonian simulation (aka the LMR protocol)
[
13
]
, as well as the Hadamard test, in order to evaluate the gradient approximation as defined in (
B.57
).
In order to hence derive the query complexity for this algorithm, we only need to multiply the cost of the number of factors we need to evaluate with the query complexity of these routines.
For this we will rely on the following result.

Theorem 17

(Sample based Hamiltonian simulation
[
57
]
)
.

Let
0\leq\epsilon_{h}\leq 1/6
be an error parameter and let
\rho
be a density for which we can obtain multiple copies through queries to a oracle
O_{\rho}
.
We can then simulate the time evolution
e^{-i\rho t}
up to error
\epsilon_{h}
in trace norm as long as
\epsilon_{h}/t\leq 1/(6\pi)
with
\Theta(t^{2}/\epsilon_{h})
copies of
\rho
and hence queries to
O_{\rho}
.

We in particularly need to evaluate terms of the form

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho e^{\frac{is\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}e^{\frac{i\pi m^{\prime}}{2}\sigma_{v}(\theta_{j})}e^{\frac{i(1-s)\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}\right)

(B.97)

Note that we can simulate every term in the trace (except
\rho
) via the sample based Hamiltonian simulation approach to error
\epsilon_{h}
in trace norm.
This will introduce a additional error which we need to take into account for the analysis.
Let
\tilde{U}_{i},i\in\{1,2,3\}
be the unitaries such that
\left\lVert U_{i}-\tilde{U}_{i}\right\rVert_{*}\leq\epsilon_{h}
where the
U_{i}
are corresponding to the factors in (
B.97
), i.e.,

U_{1}:=e^{\frac{is\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}
,
U_{2}:=e^{\frac{i\pi m^{\prime}}{2}\sigma_{v}(\theta_{j})}
, and
U_{3}:=e^{\frac{i(1-s)\pi m}{2}\sigma_{v}}
.
We can then bound the error as follows. First note that
\left\lVert\tilde{U}_{i}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\tilde{U}_{i}-U_{i}\right\rVert+\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert\leq 1+\epsilon_{h}
, using Theorem
17
and the fact that the spectral norm is upper bounded by the trace norm.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho U_{1}U_{2}U_{3}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho\tilde{U}_{1}\tilde{U}_{2}\tilde{U}_{3}\right)\leq

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\rho U_{1}U_{2}U_{3}-\rho\tilde{U}_{1}\tilde{U}_{2}\tilde{U}_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert U_{1}U_{2}U_{3}-\tilde{U}_{1}\tilde{U}_{2}\tilde{U}_{3}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert U_{1}-\tilde{U}_{1}\right\rVert\left\lVert\tilde{U}_{2}\right\rVert\left\lVert\tilde{U}_{3}\right\rVert+\left\lVert U_{2}-\tilde{U}_{2}\right\rVert\tilde{U_{3}}+\left\lVert U_{3}-\tilde{U}_{3}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert U_{1}-\tilde{U}_{1}\right\rVert_{*}(1+\epsilon_{h})^{2}+\left\lVert U_{2}-\tilde{U}_{2}\right\rVert_{*}(1+\epsilon_{h})+\left\lVert U_{3}-\tilde{U}_{3}\right\rVert_{*}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{h}(1+\epsilon_{h})^{2}+\epsilon_{h}(1+\epsilon_{h})+\epsilon_{h}=O(\epsilon_{h}),

(B.98)

neglecting higher orders of
\epsilon_{h}
, and where in the first step we applied the Von-Neumann trace inequality and the fact that
\rho
is Hermitian, and in the last step we used the results of Theorem
17
.
We hence require
O((\max\{M_{1},M_{2}\}\pi)^{2}/\epsilon_{h})
queries to the oracles for
\sigma_{v}
for the evaluation of each term in the multi sum in (
B.57
).
Note that the Hadamard test has a query cost of
O(1)
.
In order to hence achieve an overall error of
\epsilon
in the gradient estimation we require the error introduced by the sample based Hamiltonian simulation also to be of
O(\epsilon)
.
In order to do so we require
\epsilon_{h}\leq O(\frac{\epsilon\Delta}{5\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}M_{1}\pi\mu^{2}\log(\mu/2)})
, similar to the sample based error which yield the query complexity of

O\left(\frac{\max\{M_{1},M_{2}\}^{2}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}M_{1}\pi^{3}\mu^{2}\log(\mu/2)}{\epsilon\Delta}\right)

(B.99)

Adjusting the constants gives then the required bound of
\epsilon
of the total error and the query complexity for the algorithm to the Gibbs state preparation procedure is consequentially given by the number of terms in (
B.57
) times the query complexity for the individual term, yielding

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{M_{1}^{2}M_{2}\max\{M_{1},M_{2}\}^{2}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\sigma_{m}\pi^{3}\mu^{3}\log\left(\frac{\mu}{2}\right)}{\epsilon\ \epsilon_{s}^{2}\Delta}\right),

(B.100)

and classical precomputation polynomial in
M_{1},M_{2},K_{1},L,s,\Delta,\mu
, where the different quantities are defines in eq. (
B.89
-
B.96
).

Taking into account the query complexity of the individual steps then results in Theorem
13
.
We proceed by proving this theorem next.

Proof of Theorem
13
.

The runtime follows straight forward by using the bounds derived in (
B.99
) and Lemma
21
, and by using the bounds for the parameters
M_{1},M_{2},K_{1},L,\mu,\Delta,s
given in eq. (
B.89
-
B.96
).
For the success probability for estimating the whole gradient with dimensionality
d
, we can now again make use of the boosting scheme used in (
B.23
) to be

\displaystyle\tilde{O}\left(\frac{d\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}^{3}\sigma_{m}^{3}\mu^{5}\log^{3}(\mu/2)\mathrm{polylog}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert}{\epsilon},\,\frac{n_{h}^{2}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\sigma_{m}}{\epsilon\Delta}\right)}{\epsilon^{3}\Delta^{3}}\log\left(d\right)\right),

(B.101)

where
\mu=n_{h}+\log(M_{1}\Lambda/\epsilon)
.

Next we need to take into account the errors from the Gibbs state preparation given in Lemma
17
.
For this note that the error between the perfect Hamiltonian simulation of
\sigma_{v}
and the sample based Hamiltonian simulation with an erroneous density matrix denoted by
\tilde{U}
, i.e., including the error from the Gibbs state preparation procedure, is given by

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{U}-e^{-i\sigma_{v}t}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\tilde{U}-e^{-i\tilde{\sigma}_{v}t}\right\rVert+\left\lVert e^{-i\tilde{\sigma}_{v}t}-e^{-i\sigma_{v}t}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{h}+\epsilon_{G}t

(B.102)

where
\epsilon_{h}
is the error of the sample based Hamiltonian simulation, which holds since the trace norm is an upper bound for the spectral norm, and
\left\lVert\sigma_{v}-\tilde{\sigma}_{v}\right\rVert\leq\epsilon_{G}
is the error for the Gibbs state preparation from Theorem
15
for a
d
-sparse Hamiltonian, for a cost

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{N}{z}}\left\lVert H\right\rVert d\log\left(\frac{\left\lVert H\right\rVert}{\epsilon_{G}}\right)\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{G}}\right)\right).

From (
B.3.1
) we know that the error
\epsilon_{h}
propagates nearly linear, and hence it suffices for us to take
\epsilon_{G}\leq\epsilon_{h}/t
where
t=O(\max\{M_{1},M_{2}\})
and adjust the constants
\epsilon_{h}\leftarrow\epsilon_{h}/2
in order to achieve the same precision
\epsilon
in the final result.
We hence require

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{N}{z}}\left\lVert H(\theta)\right\rVert\log\left(\frac{\left\lVert H(\theta)\right\rVert\max\{M_{1},M_{2}\}}{\epsilon_{h}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\max\{M_{1},M_{2}\}}{\epsilon_{h}}\right)\right)

(B.103)

and using the
\epsilon_{h}
from before we hence find that this s bound by

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{N}{z}}\left\lVert H(\theta)\right\rVert\log\left(\frac{\left\lVert H(\theta)\right\rVert n_{h}^{2}}{\epsilon\Delta}\right)\log\left(\frac{n_{h}^{2}}{\epsilon\Delta}\right)\right)

(B.104)

query complexity to the oracle of
H
for the Gibbs state preparation.

The procedure succeeds with probability at least
1-\delta_{s}
for a single repetition for each entry of the gradient.
In order to have a failure probability of the final algorithm of less than
1/3
, we need to repeat the procedure for all
D
dimensions of the gradient and take for each the median over a number of samples.
Let
n_{f}
be as previously the number of instances of the one component of the gradient such that the error is larger than
\epsilon_{s}\sigma_{m}
and
n_{s}
be the number of instances with an error
\leq\epsilon_{s}\sigma_{m}
, and the result that we take is the median of the estimates, where we take
n=n_{s}+n_{f}
samples.
The algorithm gives a wrong answer for each dimension if
n_{s}\leq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor
, since then the median is a sample such that the error is larger than
\epsilon_{s}\sigma_{m}
.
Let
p=1-\delta_{s}
be the success probability to draw a positive sample, as is the case of our algorithm.
Since each instance of (recall that each sample here consists of a number of samples itself) from the algorithm will independently return an estimate for the entry of the gradient, the total failure probability is bounded by the union bound, i.e.,

{\rm Pr}_{fail}\leq D\cdot{\rm Pr}\left[n_{s}\leq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor\right]\leq D\cdot e^{-\frac{n}{2(1-\delta_{s})}\left((1-\delta_{s})-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{3},

(B.105)

which follows from the Chernoff inequality for a binomial variable with
1-\delta_{s}>1/2
, which is given in our case for a proper choice of
\delta_{s}<1/2
.
Therefore, by taking
n\geq\frac{2-2\delta_{s}}{(1/2-\delta_{s})^{2}}\log(3D)=O(\log(3D))
, we achieve a total failure probability of at least
1/3
for a constant, fixed
\delta_{s}
.
Note that this hence results in an multiplicative factor of
O(\log(D))
in the query complexity of (
5.25
).

The total query complexity to the oracle
O_{\rho}
for a purified density matrix of the data
\rho
and the Hamiltonian oracle
O_{H}
is then given by

\tilde{O}\left(\sqrt{\frac{N}{z}}\frac{d\log\left(d\right)\left\lVert H(\theta)\right\rVert\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}^{3}\sigma_{m}^{3}\mu^{5}\log^{3}(\mu/2)\mathrm{polylog}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\frac{\partial\sigma_{v}}{\partial\theta}\right\rVert}{\epsilon},\,\frac{n_{h}^{2}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{1}\sigma_{m}}{\epsilon\Delta},\,\left\lVert H(\theta)\right\rVert\right)}{\epsilon^{3}\Delta^{3}}\right),

(B.106)

which reduces to

\displaystyle\tilde{O}\left(\sqrt{\frac{N}{z}}\frac{D\left\lVert H(\theta)\right\rVert d\mu^{5}\alpha}{\epsilon^{3}}\right),

(B.107)

hiding the logarithmic factors in the
\tilde{O}
notation.
∎
